It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.

Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.

It’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.

Even little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.

So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I lie there replaying client models in my head, like I’m back at my desk trying to figure out what I missed, and then my brain won’t shut off.

And then even if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work. It’s ridiculous because there’s nothing I can actually fix at that hour—though sometimes I’ll start mentally drafting emails, which of course makes it worse.

So I guess it’s not one single thing. It’s that work pressure and being overwhelmed have gotten into my sleep, and now I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.

That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think that’s exactly it. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Probably the client models, if I had to pick one thing. I keep thinking I’ve missed something in them, or that the assumptions are wrong, and then it follows me home.

I’ll be lying there and my mind goes straight to work—client models, numbers, whether I missed something obvious. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to function the next day, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, like I’m already bracing for the office. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think the environment is part of it, but not just like, the lighting or the office layout or something. It’s more the constant pressure around the client models—everyone needing numbers, revisions, another version, another assumption checked.

And then it sort of follows me home. I’ll get into bed and I’m still replaying the models in my head, like I’m trying to catch some mistake. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work.

It reminds me of when I used to study late in college and couldn’t turn my brain off, except now it’s not an exam, it’s… every day. Then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and the office just starts the whole loop again.

I’ll get into bed and my mind goes straight back to client models and numbers and what I might’ve missed, and then even if I do drift off, I’m up at like 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be the person who could sleep anywhere—airport chairs, those awful hotel pillows when I traveled for work—but now I go into the office feeling like I never really rested at all. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think that’s exactly it. I’ll be lying there and my mind goes straight to work—client models, numbers, whether I missed something obvious. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to function the next day, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, like I’m already bracing for the office. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble doing everyday tasks at home because you're tired?\n\nPatient: At home… yes, though I notice it more in the things outside work, if that makes sense. I let things slide. I’ve stopped showing up for the volunteer teaching, yoga, and book club—those were the things that made my life feel like it wasn’t just spreadsheets and takeout containers.\n\nAnd work has been affected too. I got a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping, which is… awful. So yes, everyday functioning is harder. I don’t feel like I’m keeping up with much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you think is causing your sleep problems or is it just a general feeling of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular work project or situation that you think is contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client models, I think. I think it’s more the overall feeling of being overwhelmed, though the deadlines make it worse. It’s not like one due date passes and then I can relax.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I’m just not sleeping in any way that actually helps. I’ll get into bed and my mind goes straight back to client models and numbers and what I might’ve missed, and then even if I do drift off, I’m up at like 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be the person who could sleep anywhere—airport chairs, those awful hotel pillows when I traveled for work—but now I go into the office feeling like I never really rested at all. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think that’s exactly it. I’ll be lying there and my mind goes straight to work—client models, numbers, whether I missed something obvious. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to function the next day, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, like I’m already bracing for the office. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble doing everyday tasks at home because you're tired?\n\nPatient: At home… yes, though I notice it more in the things outside work, if that makes sense. I let things slide. I’ve stopped showing up for the volunteer teaching, yoga, and book club—those were the things that made my life feel like it wasn’t just spreadsheets and takeout containers.\n\nAnd work has been affected too. I got a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping, which is… awful. So yes, everyday functioning is harder. I don’t feel like I’m keeping up with much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you think is causing your sleep problems or is it just a general feeling of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I lie there replaying client models in my head, like I’m back at my desk trying to figure out what I missed, and then my brain won’t shut off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work. It’s ridiculous because there’s nothing I can actually fix at that hour—though sometimes I’ll start mentally drafting emails, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one single thing. It’s that work pressure and being overwhelmed have gotten into my sleep, and now I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular work project or situation that you think is contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client models, I think. I mean, not one neat project with a name I can point to—there are a few—but I’ll get into bed and my mind is still at work, replaying assumptions or numbers or what I might have missed.\n\nAnd then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking through it again, like somehow I’m going to solve it half-asleep. I’ll lie there replaying the models in my head, and then I’m awake again at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back at work mentally. It’s like my brain opens the spreadsheet before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, which makes the whole thing feel even more… impossible.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your work environment that you think is triggering your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the environment is part of it, but not just like, the lighting or the office layout or something. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble doing everyday tasks at home because you're tired?\n\nPatient: At home… yes, though I notice it more in the things outside work, if that makes sense. I let things slide. I’ve stopped showing up for the volunteer teaching, yoga, and book club—those were the things that made my life feel like it wasn’t just spreadsheets and takeout containers.\n\nAnd work has been affected too. I got a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping, which is… awful. So yes, everyday functioning is harder. I don’t feel like I’m keeping up with much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you think is causing your sleep problems or is it just a general feeling of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I lie there replaying client models in my head, like I’m back at my desk trying to figure out what I missed, and then my brain won’t shut off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work. It’s ridiculous because there’s nothing I can actually fix at that hour—though sometimes I’ll start mentally drafting emails, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one single thing. It’s that work pressure and being overwhelmed have gotten into my sleep, and now I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular work project or situation that you think is contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client models, I think. I mean, not one neat project with a name I can point to—there are a few—but I’ll get into bed and my mind is still at work, replaying assumptions or numbers or what I might have missed.\n\nAnd then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking through it again, like somehow I’m going to solve it half-asleep. Which I don’t. I just go to work feeling like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work that you think is causing you the most stress?\n\nPatient: Probably the client models, if I had to pick one thing. I keep thinking I’ve missed something in them, or that the assumptions are wrong, and then it follows me home.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the models in my head, and then I’m awake again at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back at work mentally. It’s like my brain opens the spreadsheet before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, which makes the whole thing feel even more… impossible.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your work environment that you think is triggering your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the environment is part of it, but not just like, the lighting or the office layout or something. It’s more the constant pressure around the client models—everyone needing numbers, revisions, another version, another assumption checked.\n\nAnd then it sort of follows me home. I’ll get into bed and I’m still replaying the models in my head, like I’m trying to catch some mistake. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to study late in college and couldn’t turn my brain off, except now it’s not an exam, it’s… every day. Then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and the office just starts the whole loop again.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping more related to the pressure of meeting specific work deadlines or the overall feeling of being overwhelmed with your job?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the overall feeling of being overwhelmed, though the deadlines make it worse. It’s not like one due date passes and then I can relax.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the client models, like I’m back at my desk, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight. It’s strange, I’ll sometimes remember doing that in college before exams, making lists in my head instead of sleeping, but now it’s just… work, all the time.\n\nThen I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and of course that makes the models feel even harder to face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have trouble sleeping if you were not working in finance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, maybe it wouldn’t be exactly this specific if I weren’t in finance, because it’s the client models that get stuck in my head. I’ll lie there replaying them, like I’m checking assumptions over and over.\n\nBut I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and then I go to work feeling like I never actually rested. So… maybe a different job would change the content, I guess, but right now finance is what my brain won’t let go of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble doing everyday tasks at home because you're tired?\n\nPatient: At home… yes, though I notice it more in the things outside work, if that makes sense. I let things slide. I’ve stopped showing up for the volunteer teaching, yoga, and book club—those were the things that made my life feel like it wasn’t just spreadsheets and takeout containers.\n\nAnd work has been affected too. I got a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping, which is… awful. So yes, everyday functioning is harder. I don’t feel like I’m keeping up with much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you think is causing your sleep problems or is it just a general feeling of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I lie there replaying client models in my head, like I’m back at my desk trying to figure out what I missed, and then my brain won’t shut off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work. It’s ridiculous because there’s nothing I can actually fix at that hour—though sometimes I’ll start mentally drafting emails, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one single thing. It’s that work pressure and being overwhelmed have gotten into my sleep, and now I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular work project or situation that you think is contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client models, I think. I mean, not one neat project with a name I can point to—there are a few—but I’ll get into bed and my mind is still at work, replaying assumptions or numbers or what I might have missed.\n\nAnd then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking through it again, like somehow I’m going to solve it half-asleep. Which I don’t. I just go to work feeling like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work that you think is causing you the most stress?\n\nPatient: Probably the client models, if I had to pick one thing. I keep thinking I’ve missed something in them, or that the assumptions are wrong, and then it follows me home.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the models in my head, and then I’m awake again at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back at work mentally. It’s like my brain opens the spreadsheet before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, which makes the whole thing feel even more… impossible.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your work environment that you think is triggering your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the environment is part of it, but not just like, the lighting or the office layout or something. It’s more the constant pressure around the client models—everyone needing numbers, revisions, another version, another assumption checked.\n\nAnd then it sort of follows me home. I’ll get into bed and I’m still replaying the models in my head, like I’m trying to catch some mistake. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to study late in college and couldn’t turn my brain off, except now it’s not an exam, it’s… every day. Then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and the office just starts the whole loop again.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping more related to the pressure of meeting specific work deadlines or the overall feeling of being overwhelmed with your job?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the overall feeling of being overwhelmed, though the deadlines make it worse. It’s not like one due date passes and then I can relax.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the client models, like I’m back at my desk, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight. It’s strange, I’ll sometimes remember doing that in college before exams, making lists in my head instead of sleeping, but now it’s just… work, all the time.\n\nThen I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and of course that makes the models feel even harder to face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have trouble sleeping if you were not working in finance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, maybe it wouldn’t be exactly this specific if I weren’t in finance, because it’s the client models that get stuck in my head. I’ll lie there replaying them, like I’m checking assumptions over and over.\n\nBut I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and then I go to work feeling like I never actually rested. So… maybe a different job would change the content, I guess, but right now finance is what my brain won’t let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty sleeping would improve if you weren't worried about work?\n\nPatient: Probably some, yeah. I mean, if I weren’t lying there thinking about work, maybe my brain would have less to grab onto.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I get into bed and I’m replaying client models, checking numbers in my head like I’m still at the office. Then I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight, and by the time I actually go in, I feel like I never rested at all.\n\nIt’s funny—not funny, but… I used to have a roommate who could fall asleep with the TV on in five minutes, and I remember being jealous even then. But now it’s more like the work worry has become the whole rhythm of the night.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep would improve if your work situation changed significantly, such as getting a new job or a significant reduction in workload?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I mean, if the workload were lighter or I were somewhere else, maybe I wouldn’t be lying there running through those client models like I’m still at my desk.\n\nBut I don’t know… I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll finally get into bed and then my head starts checking assumptions and numbers, and then I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight. It reminds me of when my old neighbor used to leave for work before dawn and I’d hear the car start, except now I’m already awake before anything even happens.\n\nSo, yes, maybe a big change at work would help, but right now I just go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it would be fixed completely. I mean, maybe if I could shut off the work thoughts and the worry, I’d have a better chance.

But the problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I lie there replaying client models, like I’m still checking them, and then even when I do sleep, I’m awake at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight. There used to be this clock in my parents’ kitchen that clicked really loudly at night, and I remember noticing every single tick when I couldn’t sleep there… that’s kind of how it feels now, except it’s numbers instead of ticking.

So yes, maybe without the worrying I could fall asleep easier, but right now I go to work feeling like I never actually rested.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I’m just not sleeping in any way that actually helps. I’ll get into bed and my mind goes straight back to client models and numbers and what I might’ve missed, and then even if I do drift off, I’m up at like 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight.\n\nIt’s funny, I used to be the person who could sleep anywhere—airport chairs, those awful hotel pillows when I traveled for work—but now I go into the office feeling like I never really rested at all. That’s really why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're lying in bed and can't sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think that’s exactly it. I’ll be lying there and my mind goes straight to work—client models, numbers, whether I missed something obvious. And then I start thinking about how I’m going to function the next day, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, like I’m already bracing for the office. It’s ridiculous because I used to sleep through anything—hotels, airport chairs, whatever—but now it just doesn’t feel like sleep that actually helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried at times other than when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to separate it out. I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble doing everyday tasks at home because you're tired?\n\nPatient: At home… yes, though I notice it more in the things outside work, if that makes sense. I let things slide. I’ve stopped showing up for the volunteer teaching, yoga, and book club—those were the things that made my life feel like it wasn’t just spreadsheets and takeout containers.\n\nAnd work has been affected too. I got a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping, which is… awful. So yes, everyday functioning is harder. I don’t feel like I’m keeping up with much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you think is causing your sleep problems or is it just a general feeling of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I lie there replaying client models in my head, like I’m back at my desk trying to figure out what I missed, and then my brain won’t shut off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work. It’s ridiculous because there’s nothing I can actually fix at that hour—though sometimes I’ll start mentally drafting emails, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one single thing. It’s that work pressure and being overwhelmed have gotten into my sleep, and now I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular work project or situation that you think is contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client models, I think. I mean, not one neat project with a name I can point to—there are a few—but I’ll get into bed and my mind is still at work, replaying assumptions or numbers or what I might have missed.\n\nAnd then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking through it again, like somehow I’m going to solve it half-asleep. Which I don’t. I just go to work feeling like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work that you think is causing you the most stress?\n\nPatient: Probably the client models, if I had to pick one thing. I keep thinking I’ve missed something in them, or that the assumptions are wrong, and then it follows me home.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the models in my head, and then I’m awake again at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back at work mentally. It’s like my brain opens the spreadsheet before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, which makes the whole thing feel even more… impossible.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your work environment that you think is triggering your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the environment is part of it, but not just like, the lighting or the office layout or something. It’s more the constant pressure around the client models—everyone needing numbers, revisions, another version, another assumption checked.\n\nAnd then it sort of follows me home. I’ll get into bed and I’m still replaying the models in my head, like I’m trying to catch some mistake. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to study late in college and couldn’t turn my brain off, except now it’s not an exam, it’s… every day. Then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and the office just starts the whole loop again.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping more related to the pressure of meeting specific work deadlines or the overall feeling of being overwhelmed with your job?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the overall feeling of being overwhelmed, though the deadlines make it worse. It’s not like one due date passes and then I can relax.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the client models, like I’m back at my desk, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight. It’s strange, I’ll sometimes remember doing that in college before exams, making lists in my head instead of sleeping, but now it’s just… work, all the time.\n\nThen I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and of course that makes the models feel even harder to face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have trouble sleeping if you were not working in finance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Yeah… I think so. It really started after the merger, when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. That was, what, about three years ago now.

I notice it most at night because everything gets quiet, but during the day I’m usually just moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI guess at work there’s always some pressure, but that’s sort of the job, you know? Finance isn’t exactly a calm environment. I had this old manager who used to say if you’re relaxed, you probably missed something, which is a terrible thing to have stuck in your head. So… maybe, but I don’t always recognize it as “worry” in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work or trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a way I can point to clearly. When I’m not at work or in bed, I’m usually doing errands or half-watching something, and I’m not sure I’d call it being “on edge.”\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be in the grocery store and remember I forgot to buy coffee filters—actually, I still have this reusable one my sister got me that never works right—and then I sort of spiral into making a list in my head, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just… being tired and scattered. So I guess I haven’t really noticed it the same way outside of work and nighttime.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about work or other things when you're doing everyday activities like grocery shopping?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes in a clean way. Like, if I’m grocery shopping, I might suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer, but then I’ll also be trying to remember whether I already have onions at home, and then I’m annoyed because the store moved everything around again.\n\nSo… I don’t know if that counts as worrying about work, exactly. My mind wanders there sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it. It all kind of blends together.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping only related to work-related thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: Not only, no. Work is the main thing my brain grabs onto—client models, numbers, whether I missed something—but the bigger problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore.\n\nI’ll lie there replaying work stuff, and then even if I do fall asleep, I wake up at like 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight. And then I’m up, or half-up, and by the time I go to work I feel like I never actually rested. It’s not just one thought, exactly. It’s like my body didn’t get the message that the day ended.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to concentrate or perform tasks at work or in your daily life because of your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At work especially. Spreadsheets that used to be kind of automatic for me now take forever. I’ll be looking at the same set of assumptions and then realize I’ve reread the same line, I don’t know, five times, and I still don’t feel like I’ve actually taken it in.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because this is what I do. I used to be able to hold the whole model in my head, like where the numbers were coming from, what needed to tie out… now I lose the thread. And then before a presentation I’ll sit there checking and rechecking because I’m worried I’ve missed something obvious.\n\nEven little things, like formatting a table—well, our templates are ridiculous anyway, they changed the font again and nobody told us—but yes, the concentration part is definitely worse when I haven’t slept.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it harder to enjoy or participate in activities you normally like to do because of how you're sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have, and I hate admitting that because those things were sort of the scaffolding that kept me from just being work-home-work-home.\n\nI used to volunteer teach, and I’ve basically stopped showing up. Same with yoga, and my book club. I tell myself I’m just tired or I’ll go next week, but then next week comes and I don’t go. And with work slipping too— I mean, I got a formal performance warning recently because my models and presentations keep having problems, which is… humiliating.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about activities I enjoy. Yes. I’m not really participating in them anymore. Sleep is a big part of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble doing everyday tasks at home because you're tired?\n\nPatient: At home… yes, though I notice it more in the things outside work, if that makes sense. I let things slide. I’ve stopped showing up for the volunteer teaching, yoga, and book club—those were the things that made my life feel like it wasn’t just spreadsheets and takeout containers.\n\nAnd work has been affected too. I got a formal performance warning because my models and presentations keep slipping, which is… awful. So yes, everyday functioning is harder. I don’t feel like I’m keeping up with much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you think is causing your sleep problems or is it just a general feeling of being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… partly specific, partly just everything piling up. I lie there replaying client models in my head, like I’m back at my desk trying to figure out what I missed, and then my brain won’t shut off.\n\nAnd then even if I do fall asleep, I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work. It’s ridiculous because there’s nothing I can actually fix at that hour—though sometimes I’ll start mentally drafting emails, which of course makes it worse.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one single thing. It’s that work pressure and being overwhelmed have gotten into my sleep, and now I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular work project or situation that you think is contributing to your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the client models, I think. I mean, not one neat project with a name I can point to—there are a few—but I’ll get into bed and my mind is still at work, replaying assumptions or numbers or what I might have missed.\n\nAnd then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight, already thinking through it again, like somehow I’m going to solve it half-asleep. Which I don’t. I just go to work feeling like I never really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular thing at work that you think is causing you the most stress?\n\nPatient: Probably the client models, if I had to pick one thing. I keep thinking I’ve missed something in them, or that the assumptions are wrong, and then it follows me home.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the models in my head, and then I’m awake again at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, already back at work mentally. It’s like my brain opens the spreadsheet before I even get out of bed.\n\nAnd then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, which makes the whole thing feel even more… impossible.\n\nDoctor: Is there something about your work environment that you think is triggering your sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think the environment is part of it, but not just like, the lighting or the office layout or something. It’s more the constant pressure around the client models—everyone needing numbers, revisions, another version, another assumption checked.\n\nAnd then it sort of follows me home. I’ll get into bed and I’m still replaying the models in my head, like I’m trying to catch some mistake. I barely sleep in any way that feels useful anymore. I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight, already thinking about work.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to study late in college and couldn’t turn my brain off, except now it’s not an exam, it’s… every day. Then I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and the office just starts the whole loop again.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty sleeping more related to the pressure of meeting specific work deadlines or the overall feeling of being overwhelmed with your job?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the overall feeling of being overwhelmed, though the deadlines make it worse. It’s not like one due date passes and then I can relax.\n\nI came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll lie there replaying the client models, like I’m back at my desk, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach tight. It’s strange, I’ll sometimes remember doing that in college before exams, making lists in my head instead of sleeping, but now it’s just… work, all the time.\n\nThen I go in feeling like I never actually rested, and of course that makes the models feel even harder to face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still have trouble sleeping if you were not working in finance?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, maybe it wouldn’t be exactly this specific if I weren’t in finance, because it’s the client models that get stuck in my head. I’ll lie there replaying them, like I’m checking assumptions over and over.\n\nBut I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight, and then I go to work feeling like I never actually rested. So… maybe a different job would change the content, I guess, but right now finance is what my brain won’t let go of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty sleeping would improve if you weren't worried about work?\n\nPatient: Probably some, yeah. I mean, if I weren’t lying there thinking about work, maybe my brain would have less to grab onto.\n\nBut it’s gotten to where I barely sleep in any way that feels useful. I get into bed and I’m replaying client models, checking numbers in my head like I’m still at the office. Then I’ll wake up at 3 or 4 with my stomach all tight, and by the time I actually go in, I feel like I never rested at all.\n\nIt’s funny—not funny, but… I used to have a roommate who could fall asleep with the TV on in five minutes, and I remember being jealous even then. But now it’s more like the work worry has become the whole rhythm of the night.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep would improve if your work situation changed significantly, such as getting a new job or a significant reduction in workload?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. I mean, if the workload were lighter or I were somewhere else, maybe I wouldn’t be lying there running through those client models like I’m still at my desk.\n\nBut I don’t know… I came in because I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I’ll finally get into bed and then my head starts checking assumptions and numbers, and then I’m up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my stomach all tight. It reminds me of when my old neighbor used to leave for work before dawn and I’d hear the car start, except now I’m already awake before anything even happens.\n\nSo, yes, maybe a big change at work would help, but right now I just go in feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to fall asleep or stay asleep if you weren't thinking about work or other worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it would be fixed completely. I mean, maybe if I could shut off the work thoughts and the worry, I’d have a better chance.\n\nBut the problem is I barely sleep in any useful way anymore. I lie there replaying client models, like I’m still checking them, and then even when I do sleep, I’m awake at 3 or 4 with my stomach tight. There used to be this clock in my parents’ kitchen that clicked really loudly at night, and I remember noticing every single tick when I couldn’t sleep there… that’s kind of how it feels now, except it’s numbers instead of ticking.\n\nSo yes, maybe without the worrying I could fall asleep easier, but right now I go to work feeling like I never actually rested.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel anxious or worried during the day, outside of when you're trying to sleep or thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate it, honestly. During the day I’m usually doing something, so it’s harder to tell if it’s “anxious” or just… moving from one thing to the next.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll be making coffee and realize I’ve been standing there too long, like I forgot why I was in the kitchen, but that could just be being tired. My sister always says I overthink things—she said that even when we were kids, like if we were choosing a movie I’d want to know the whole plan first. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I notice it most when things get quiet, like at night. During the day, I guess I haven’t paid as much attention to whether it’s worry or just the day happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your sleep problems started after a significant change or stressor in your life, such as a change at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It really started after the merger, when they reorganized our Caribbean-market division. That was, what, about three years ago now.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the usual adjustment period—new reporting lines, new people, everyone acting like the new spreadsheet format was the most important thing in the world. I remember they changed the conference room names too, which is silly, but I kept going to the wrong one for weeks.\n\nBut the sleep problems really have been going on since around then, about three years.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
